Table 1
The Power of Questioning: Inquiry, Innovation, and Change
1. Q: What does questioning mean beyond simply asking people for information?
A: Show answer
Questioning is a way of organizing thought around what is not yet known. It can obtain facts, but its wider value is that it exposes gaps in understanding, tests assumptions, reframes problems, and opens possibilities for action. A well-chosen question can help a person recognize that an accepted practice is not inevitable, that a problem has been defined too narrowly, or that an alternative explanation deserves examination. In this sense, questioning is not merely a conversational technique. It is a method for learning, innovation, problem-solving, critical reflection, and personal or organizational change.
Study instruction:
- Identify one situation in which a question could change how the problem is understood, rather than merely obtain another fact.
Key point:
- Questions organize thinking around uncertainty and missing knowledge.
- Their value includes reframing problems and enabling change.
2. Q: Why does questioning become especially important during periods of uncertainty and rapid change?
A: Show answer
Uncertainty makes inherited assumptions less reliable because conditions, technologies, work patterns, and social expectations may change faster than established routines. Questions help people identify what is no longer known, which expectations need to be reconsidered, and what new information is required before making decisions. During a disruption, a rule that once seemed permanent may prove temporary or context-dependent. That realization can encourage people to examine other assumptions as well. Questioning does not remove uncertainty or guarantee a correct answer; it provides a disciplined way to explore possibilities, learn, and adapt without pretending that the future is already understood.
Study instruction:
- Choose a recent disruption and list the assumptions it challenged, the questions it raised, and the decisions that required new evidence.
Key point:
- Change weakens the reliability of unexamined assumptions.
- Inquiry helps people adapt while important answers remain uncertain.
3. Q: How can a question help someone organize thinking around what they do not know?
A: Show answer
A question turns a vague sense of uncertainty into a more specific information gap. Instead of saying that a project is failing, for example, a team might ask which assumption about user needs, resources, timing, or implementation has not been tested. The question identifies what must be investigated and helps distinguish known facts from unknown conditions. It also provides a boundary for inquiry: not every possible fact is relevant, but information that can answer the question is. A useful question therefore gives curiosity direction and makes it easier to decide what to observe, research, discuss, or test next.
Study instruction:
- Convert a vague concern into three specific questions, then identify the evidence needed to answer each one.
Key point:
- A precise question makes an information gap visible.
- It directs attention toward evidence that can reduce relevant uncertainty.
Source links:
-
- The Multifaceted Role of Self-Generated Question Asking in Curiosity-Driven Learning
4. Q: How does questioning contribute to innovation and breakthrough ideas?
A: Show answer
Innovation often begins when someone questions a convention, an accepted explanation, a customer assumption, or a routine that others have stopped examining. A question can reveal an unmet need, expose an inefficient process, or suggest that a familiar problem could be approached from a different direction. Questioning is not sufficient by itself: an idea must also be explored, tested, refined, and implemented. Research on the Innovator's DNA identifies questioning alongside associating, observing, experimenting, and networking as discovery skills associated with innovative entrepreneurs and executives. This supports the chapter's central point that useful questions can initiate a wider process of discovery rather than produce innovation automatically.
Study instruction:
- Select an existing product or process and write questions that challenge its purpose, users, assumptions, and current method of delivery.
Key point:
- Innovation can begin by challenging what is treated as unquestionable.
- Questions work best when connected to observation, experimentation, and action.
Source links:
-
- INSEAD: The Innovator's DNA
5. Q: What is a beautiful question in the sense used by Warren Berger?
A: Show answer
A beautiful question is an ambitious yet actionable question that can shift how a person perceives or thinks about something and may become a catalyst for change. It is ambitious because it challenges an existing frame rather than accepting the first explanation. It is actionable because it points toward an inquiry that can be explored through evidence, conversation, observation, or experimentation. The question need not be elegant in wording. Its quality comes from the way it opens a meaningful possibility while remaining connected to a problem or opportunity that someone can investigate. The definition therefore joins imagination with practical direction.
Study instruction:
- Write one ambitious question about a real challenge and revise it until it suggests a practical line of investigation.
Key point:
- A beautiful question changes perspective while remaining actionable.
- Its purpose is to open a path toward meaningful change.
6. Q: How does the Why/What If/How model guide a journey of inquiry?
A: Show answer
The model organizes inquiry into three connected movements. Why questions step back from the apparent problem and examine its causes, purpose, assumptions, and context. What If questions open alternative possibilities and invite people to imagine a different approach without prematurely rejecting ideas. How questions move toward action by asking what could be tested, designed, changed, or implemented. The stages are related rather than rigid: evidence from a How experiment may reveal a new Why question, while an unexpected possibility may require further investigation. The model is a practical guide for moving from reframing to exploration and then toward an actionable response.
Study instruction:
- Apply all three stages to one challenge and write at least two questions for each stage before choosing a next action.
Key point:
- Why reframes, What If expands possibilities, and How develops action.
- Inquiry may move back and forth as new evidence emerges.
Source links:
-
- Stanford d.school: How Might We Questions
7. Q: Why should inquiry often begin with a Why question?
A: Show answer
A Why question creates distance from the first description of a problem. It asks what is producing the situation, why the issue matters, whose needs are affected, and which assumptions have shaped the current response. Without this step, people may optimize a process that should be redesigned or solve a symptom while leaving the underlying cause untouched. Why questions can also reveal whether the stated objective is still relevant. They should be asked with genuine curiosity rather than as accusations, because a hostile or repetitive why can make people defensive. The useful purpose is explanation and reframing, not blame.
Study instruction:
- Take a recurring problem and ask successive Why questions until you identify a testable underlying cause or an assumption requiring evidence.
Key point:
- Why questions examine causes, purpose, and the framing of a problem.
- They are most useful when they invite explanation rather than assign blame.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
8. Q: What is the purpose of the What If stage in the inquiry model?
A: Show answer
What If questions create space for alternatives before a team commits to the familiar solution. They encourage people to imagine different users, processes, technologies, partnerships, constraints, or outcomes. This stage helps counter the tendency to treat current arrangements as the only realistic options. Possibilities should not be accepted as facts simply because they are interesting; they become candidates for investigation. A useful What If question is open enough to generate more than one response but focused enough to remain connected to the challenge. The aim is productive exploration, not fantasy detached from the problem.
Study instruction:
- Generate five plausible alternatives to a current practice and identify which assumptions each alternative would challenge.
Key point:
- What If questions widen the range of possible responses.
- Imaginative options still require later testing and evaluation.
Source links:
-
- Stanford d.school: How Might We Questions
9. Q: How does the How stage turn questioning into practical change?
A: Show answer
How questions connect an idea to the conditions required to make progress. They ask what first step is possible, what evidence would show whether an approach works, who should participate, what resources are needed, and what risks or constraints must be managed. The stage prevents inquiry from remaining an endless discussion of possibilities. It does not require a complete solution before action begins; a small, reversible test may be enough to learn whether an idea deserves further investment. The quality of a How question is therefore judged partly by whether it leads to a useful next step and a way to learn from the result.
Study instruction:
- Convert one promising What If idea into a small test with an owner, evidence requirement, time limit, and review decision.
Key point:
- How questions translate possibilities into testable action.
- A small experiment can create evidence for the next decision.
Source links:
-
- Stanford d.school: Human-Centered Design Project Guide
10. Q: Why is the Why/What If/How model a framework rather than a fixed formula?
A: Show answer
Questioning is too dependent on context, evidence, people, and uncertainty to be reduced to a guaranteed sequence. The model offers a useful progression, but real inquiry includes detours, failed tests, unexpected discoveries, and questions that must be revised. A team may return to Why after a prototype reveals that it misunderstood the user. A What If idea may lead to a new question about feasibility before any How plan is possible. The framework is valuable because it helps people recognize different kinds of inquiry and avoid jumping directly from an incomplete problem statement to a preferred solution. It does not replace judgment.
Study instruction:
- Review a problem-solving project and mark where the team had to revisit its original question after learning something new.
Key point:
- The model guides inquiry but does not guarantee a linear process.
- New evidence may require questions to be reframed or revisited.
11. Q: What makes a question both ambitious and actionable?
A: Show answer
An ambitious question reaches beyond a minor adjustment and invites a meaningful change in perspective, practice, or outcome. An actionable question remains specific enough that a person can investigate it. For example, asking how to make an entire industry perfect is ambitious but too broad to guide a practical inquiry. Asking how a local service can reduce the time users spend completing one difficult step is narrower and can lead to observation, measurement, and testing. The balance depends on the scale of the challenge. A strong question creates room for discovery while identifying a useful boundary for the next investigation.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite one overly broad question and one trivial question so that each becomes meaningful, focused, and capable of investigation.
Key point:
- Ambition creates the potential for a new perspective.
- Actionability gives the inquiry a practical direction.
Source links:
-
- Stanford d.school: How Might We Questions
12. Q: How is an actionable question different from a grand philosophical question?
A: Show answer
A grand philosophical question may explore meaning, existence, morality, or ultimate truth and can be valuable even when no practical answer is available. An actionable question is designed to guide investigation or change in a particular situation. Berger's chapter distinguishes the book's focus from questions such as why people exist, not because those questions lack importance, but because they do not necessarily fit the book's emphasis on tangible results. For example, a philosophical question about the nature of fairness differs from asking how a workplace promotion process could be made more transparent. The second question can lead to specific evidence and possible interventions.
Study instruction:
- Choose a broad philosophical topic and formulate a related question that could guide a practical project or decision.
Key point:
- Philosophical questions and actionable questions serve different purposes.
- The book emphasizes questions that can lead to tangible inquiry and change.
13. Q: Why should a worthwhile question be difficult enough to matter but answerable enough to investigate?
A: Show answer
A question that is too easy may produce information that does not change understanding or decisions. A question that is impossibly broad or vague may leave the investigator without a workable starting point. The chapter uses a physicist's description of seeking a question that is hard and interesting enough to be worth answering but sufficiently manageable to be answerable. This balance helps define the appropriate level of challenge. The investigator should preserve the significance of the issue while narrowing the scope, clarifying terms, and identifying evidence that could reasonably be gathered. A question can remain open-ended without being unbounded.
Study instruction:
- Assess a proposed research or improvement question for significance, scope, available evidence, and a realistic path to an answer.
Key point:
- A strong question has intellectual value and a feasible investigative path.
- Scope should be narrowed without removing the importance of the issue.
14. Q: How can questioning expose assumptions that people have stopped noticing?
A: Show answer
Assumptions often become invisible when a practice is repeated successfully or when people inherit it from a respected authority. A question such as what evidence supports the current method, who benefits from it, or what would happen if one condition changed makes the assumption explicit. The investigator can then test whether it is still valid, whether it applies to all situations, and whether an alternative explanation fits the evidence better. Questioning does not mean rejecting every established practice. It means distinguishing evidence-based conclusions from habits, traditions, preferences, and beliefs that have not been examined recently.
Study instruction:
- List three assumptions behind a familiar process and identify evidence that could confirm, qualify, or disprove each one.
Key point:
- Repeated practice can make assumptions appear like facts.
- Questions make hidden assumptions available for examination.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
15. Q: How can a crisis make people more willing to reconsider established assumptions?
A: Show answer
A crisis can disrupt routines that previously seemed permanent. When people discover that work, communication, services, or social arrangements can operate differently, they may become more open to examining other assumptions. The chapter presents this as a research-informed possibility rather than a guarantee that every person changes their mind after a crisis. Some people may adapt, while others may seek to restore familiar arrangements or become more defensive. The practical lesson is to use disruption as an opportunity to ask what should be preserved, what no longer works, and what new evidence should guide decisions instead of treating the past as the only model for the future.
Study instruction:
- Identify one assumption that changed during a major disruption and assess whether the new practice should continue, be modified, or be abandoned.
Key point:
- Disruption can reveal that established arrangements are not inevitable.
- Reconsideration should be guided by evidence, not novelty alone.
16. Q: How can people use questioning to define a new normal after major change?
A: Show answer
A new normal should not be assumed to be a simple return to previous arrangements or an automatic acceptance of every emergency adaptation. People need to ask which activities still serve their goals, which constraints have changed, what new opportunities exist, and what trade-offs are acceptable. The chapter illustrates this with questions about where and how long people work, how technology may affect jobs, and whether previous priorities still fit their lives. These are personal and contextual decisions, not questions with one universal answer. Inquiry helps people make deliberate choices instead of allowing old routines or temporary reactions to determine the future by default.
Study instruction:
- Review one changed work or life arrangement and write questions about purpose, benefits, costs, affected people, and long-term suitability.
Key point:
- A new normal requires deliberate choices about changed conditions and priorities.
- Old routines should be assessed rather than automatically restored.
17. Q: What does the chapter's example of young children asking many questions illustrate?
A: Show answer
The example illustrates that spontaneous curiosity and question generation can be highly visible in early childhood, while the chapter raises concern that many people become less likely to question as they grow older. It cites a study reporting a very high daily question count for one group of four-year-old children, but that figure should be treated as an illustration from the cited study rather than a universal measurement of every child. The deeper point is not the exact number. It is that questioning can be natural and energetic early in life, yet later environments may shape whether people continue to use it freely and imaginatively.
Study instruction:
- Explain what the childhood example is intended to illustrate and distinguish the reported study finding from a claim about all children.
Key point:
- Early curiosity shows how readily people can generate questions.
- A single study statistic should not be generalized to every child.
18. Q: Why might questioning become less spontaneous as children move through school and adult life?
A: Show answer
The chapter raises this as an important question rather than establishing one definitive cause. Possible influences include educational environments that reward correct answers more visibly than original questions, social expectations to appear knowledgeable, workplace hierarchies, fear of criticism, and routines that leave little time for exploration. These factors may discourage people from expressing curiosity even when they still have questions. The explanation is likely to vary across individuals and institutions. A careful response should therefore investigate the local conditions rather than assume that age alone causes a decline. The practical opportunity is to design settings where curiosity is welcomed and question-formulation is practiced.
Study instruction:
- Observe a learning or work environment and identify specific practices that encourage or discourage people from asking questions.
Key point:
- The chapter presents the decline as a problem to investigate, not a settled single-cause explanation.
- Social and institutional conditions can influence whether curiosity is expressed.
Source links:
-
- Right Question Institute: What Is the QFT?
19. Q: What is the risk of an education system that emphasizes reproducing answers more than formulating questions?
A: Show answer
If learners are mainly rewarded for reproducing accepted answers, they may receive less practice identifying knowledge gaps, challenging assumptions, and deciding what information they need. This can make learning appear to be the accumulation of correct responses rather than an active process of inquiry. Students may become skilled at responding to questions supplied by others while remaining less confident about generating their own. The chapter argues that questioning is rarely taught deliberately enough. This does not mean factual knowledge or accurate answers are unimportant; it means that knowledge should also equip learners to ask better questions, investigate uncertainty, and develop independent judgment.
Study instruction:
- Review a lesson or course and identify where students generate questions, test assumptions, and decide how to investigate answers.
Key point:
- Answering supplied questions does not fully develop question-formulation skills.
- Strong learning combines knowledge acquisition with independent inquiry.
Source links:
-
- Right Question Institute: What Is the QFT?
20. Q: How can teachers deliberately encourage students to formulate their own questions?
A: Show answer
Teachers can provide a clear stimulus, invite learners to generate questions before judging or answering them, help students improve the questions, and then prioritize those most useful for the learning objective. The Question Formulation Technique provides one structured approach: learners use a question focus, follow rules for producing questions, classify and improve them, prioritize, plan next steps, and reflect on what they learned. This creates practice in divergent, convergent, and metacognitive thinking. The teacher's role is to facilitate the process without supplying all the questions or prematurely directing learners toward a preferred answer.
Study instruction:
- Design a short classroom activity using a non-question stimulus, question generation, improvement, prioritization, investigation, and reflection.
Key point:
- Question-formulation can be taught through a deliberate process.
- Learners need opportunities to generate and use their own questions.
Source links:
-
- Right Question Institute: What Is the QFT?
21. Q: What is the Question Formulation Technique and how can it strengthen inquiry?
A: Show answer
The Question Formulation Technique is a structured method developed by the Right Question Institute to help people generate, improve, prioritize, and use their own questions. It begins with a question focus, which may be a statement, image, phrase, or other stimulus rather than a question. Participants generate questions without stopping to judge or answer them, then work with the questions by identifying open and closed forms, changing one type into another, selecting priorities, planning next steps, and reflecting. The method offers a practical extension to the chapter's argument that questioning should be taught and practiced rather than left to instinct.
Study instruction:
- Practice the QFT with a work or study challenge and record the questions generated, improved, prioritized, and investigated.
Key point:
- The QFT makes question generation and improvement explicit skills.
- Its process connects divergent thinking with prioritization and reflection.
Source links:
-
- Right Question Institute: What Is the QFT?
22. Q: Why is the quality of a question more important than the number of questions asked?
A: Show answer
Generating many questions can help people explore a topic, but volume alone does not show whether the questions are relevant, clear, or useful. A long list may contain repetitions, vague prompts, or questions unrelated to the decision at hand. Quality depends on whether a question identifies a meaningful gap, fits the context, and can guide useful investigation. The chapter distinguishes the volume of online questions from thoughtful inquiry. Research on self-generated questions also examines question quality in relation to curiosity and information seeking. The practical goal is to generate broadly at first, then refine and prioritize rather than treat quantity as proof of insight.
Study instruction:
- Review a list of questions and remove duplicates, clarify vague wording, and rank the remaining questions by relevance and investigative value.
Key point:
- More questions do not automatically produce better inquiry.
- Relevant, clear questions should be refined and prioritized.
Source links:
-
- The Multifaceted Role of Self-Generated Question Asking in Curiosity-Driven Learning
23. Q: How can recognizing an information gap stimulate curiosity and further learning?
A: Show answer
Curiosity often begins when a person recognizes that something important is missing from their understanding. Formulating a question makes the gap more explicit and gives the person a reason to seek relevant information. Instead of passively receiving more material, the learner searches for an answer that addresses a specific uncertainty. A 2023 Cognitive Science study tested self-generated questions in a laboratory task and found that participants who asked more high-quality questions reported greater curiosity, were more likely to seek semantically relevant information, and showed better later recall of acquired information. The findings support the value of question-guided learning, while the study design and sample should be considered when interpreting them.
Study instruction:
- While reading a short article, record an information gap, formulate a question, search for its answer, and test what you can recall later.
Key point:
- A question can turn an information gap into a focused learning goal.
- Research supports benefits but should be interpreted within its study design.
Source links:
-
- The Multifaceted Role of Self-Generated Question Asking in Curiosity-Driven Learning
24. Q: How can self-generated questions support memory and retention?
A: Show answer
When learners formulate their own questions, they must identify what they know, what they do not know, and what information would close the gap. That process can make subsequent information more meaningful because the learner is actively looking for an answer rather than simply encountering facts. The Cognitive Science study cited above found that, in its experimental task, participants who generated more high-quality questions were more likely to retain information they later acquired. This does not mean that question generation alone guarantees durable learning. Understanding, attention, prior knowledge, relevance, and opportunities to retrieve information also matter. Questions are best used as part of an active learning process.
Study instruction:
- Before studying a topic, write questions from memory, read to answer them, then close the source and retrieve the answers without looking.
Key point:
- Self-generated questions can make learning more active and purposeful.
- Retrieval and later practice are still needed to check retention.
Source links:
-
- The Multifaceted Role of Self-Generated Question Asking in Curiosity-Driven Learning
25. Q: When should a person use open-ended questions and when should closed-ended questions be used?
A: Show answer
Closed-ended questions are useful when a specific confirmation, classification, or short factual response is needed. Open-ended questions invite explanation and can reveal reasoning, experience, or alternatives that the questioner did not anticipate. Neither type is always superior. A closed question may establish whether a deadline was met, while an open question may reveal why the deadline was missed and what could prevent recurrence. In the Question Formulation Technique, learners are encouraged to identify both forms and practice changing one into the other. Choosing the form should depend on the information required and the stage of the conversation or investigation.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite three closed questions as open questions and explain what additional information each revised question could reveal.
Key point:
- Closed questions confirm or narrow information.
- Open questions invite explanation and broader perspectives.
Source links:
-
- Right Question Institute: What Is the QFT?
26. Q: How should a person prioritize questions after generating a large list?
A: Show answer
Prioritization should reconnect the questions to the purpose of the inquiry. A person may choose those most important to a decision, most useful for research, most likely to expose a key assumption, or most capable of guiding an experiment. The priority may change depending on available time, evidence, risk, and the people affected. It is often useful to separate questions that must be answered before action from questions that can be explored later. The Right Question Institute's process explicitly includes prioritization and next steps, helping participants move from divergent idea generation to focused investigation without discarding the value of the wider list.
Study instruction:
- Rank a set of questions against a stated objective, urgency, evidence availability, and potential consequences of leaving them unanswered.
Key point:
- Prioritization aligns questions with the purpose of the inquiry.
- The most interesting question is not always the most urgent one.
Source links:
-
- Right Question Institute: What Is the QFT?
27. Q: Why do some business leaders regard questioning as inefficient?
A: Show answer
Leaders under pressure to deliver may associate questioning with delay, indecision, or criticism of work already underway. Meetings and operations can be organized around rapid execution, leaving little time to reconsider whether the chosen objective or method is appropriate. However, acting quickly on a poorly framed problem can create rework, wasted investment, or missed opportunities. The chapter describes a business culture that may value doing over examining what should be done. The appropriate response is not to question every routine decision indefinitely. It is to make time for inquiry when the stakes, uncertainty, or potential impact justify examining assumptions before committing resources.
Study instruction:
- Review a recent business decision and identify whether a short questioning phase could have prevented rework or exposed a better option.
Key point:
- Excessive pressure to act can suppress useful examination of the problem.
- Timely inquiry can improve the quality of action rather than simply delay it.
Source links:
-
- INSEAD: The Innovator's DNA
28. Q: How can questioning challenge authority and established organizational structures?
A: Show answer
A question can expose the reasoning behind a policy, decision, hierarchy, or process that has been treated as settled. It may reveal that a rule no longer fits current conditions or that people affected by a decision have information that was not considered. This can be uncomfortable because questioning may redistribute influence, require leaders to explain choices, or invite alternatives to established practices. The chapter notes that organizations and classrooms may resist questioning because it can disrupt existing systems and challenge authority. The purpose is not to reject authority automatically. It is to make decisions more transparent and allow relevant evidence and perspectives to improve them.
Study instruction:
- Identify a policy or decision that is rarely questioned and prepare neutral questions about its purpose, evidence, effects, and review process.
Key point:
- Questions can reveal the assumptions and power relationships behind established arrangements.
- Constructive challenge seeks better reasoning, not automatic opposition.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
29. Q: How can employees raise challenging questions without making the discussion unnecessarily confrontational?
A: Show answer
Employees can frame questions around the decision, evidence, risks, and intended outcome rather than attacking the competence or motives of the decision-maker. They should clarify what they understand, identify the specific uncertainty, and explain why the answer matters to the work. For example, asking what evidence supports a delivery date is more constructive than saying the plan is unrealistic. The employee should also choose an appropriate setting and channel, listen to the response, and distinguish a genuine explanation from an unresolved concern. Where the issue involves safety, misconduct, retaliation, or legal obligations, formal reporting routes may be necessary in addition to ordinary discussion.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a confrontational challenge as a neutral evidence-seeking question and identify when formal escalation would still be required.
Key point:
- Focus questions on evidence, consequences, and decisions rather than personal blame.
- Respectful phrasing does not remove the need to escalate serious concerns.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
30. Q: What five discovery skills are associated with innovative entrepreneurs and executives in the Innovator's DNA research?
A: Show answer
The five skills are questioning, associating, observing, experimenting, and networking. Questioning challenges conventional wisdom and opens new lines of inquiry. Associating connects ideas, problems, and experiences that may initially appear unrelated. Observing involves paying attention to details in how people behave and how customers, suppliers, or organizations operate. Experimenting tests possibilities through new experiences or prototypes. Networking exposes people to perspectives from individuals with different backgrounds and expertise. The chapter uses these skills to show that innovation is not explained by questioning alone. Questions become more productive when they are connected to diverse information, observation, experimentation, and relationships.
Study instruction:
- Choose an innovation example and identify evidence of each of the five discovery skills in the way the idea was developed.
Key point:
- Questioning is one of five complementary discovery skills.
- Innovation combines inquiry with connections, observation, experiments, and diverse perspectives.
Source links:
-
- INSEAD: The Innovator's DNA
31. Q: How do questioning, observing, experimenting, associating, and networking reinforce one another?
A: Show answer
A question directs attention toward something that needs to be understood. Observation supplies information about what people actually do rather than what an investigator assumes they do. Networking introduces perspectives that may reveal overlooked needs or interpretations. Associating helps connect those observations with ideas from other contexts. Experimenting tests whether a proposed explanation or solution works in practice. The process can repeat: an experiment may produce surprising evidence that leads to a better question. Treating these skills as a connected system prevents questioning from becoming abstract speculation and prevents experimentation from becoming trial and error without a clear learning objective.
Study instruction:
- Map a proposed improvement from its first question through observation, external perspectives, idea connections, testing, and revised inquiry.
Key point:
- Discovery skills create a reinforcing cycle of inquiry and learning.
- Questions help give direction to observation and experimentation.
Source links:
-
- INSEAD: The Innovator's DNA
32. Q: Why is questioning not simply the opposite of taking action?
A: Show answer
Questioning and action serve different but complementary purposes. Inquiry helps people determine whether they are solving the right problem, which assumptions require testing, and what evidence is missing. Action generates experience and information that may not be available through discussion alone. The chapter's model ends with How questions because the purpose of inquiry is often to move toward a practical response. The danger lies in either extreme: acting without understanding can waste resources, while questioning without decisions can become avoidance. A disciplined process gives inquiry an appropriate time and scope, then uses what has been learned to choose a next step.
Study instruction:
- Review a decision and identify what needed to be questioned before action and what could only be learned by taking action.
Key point:
- Inquiry improves the framing of action; action produces new evidence.
- Neither endless discussion nor unexamined execution is sufficient.
Source links:
-
- Stanford d.school: Human-Centered Design Project Guide
33. Q: How can questioning interrupt automatic or autopilot thinking?
A: Show answer
Automatic routines reduce the mental effort required to handle familiar tasks, which can be useful when people face many demands. The chapter notes that the brain often reduces workload by accepting or ignoring information and relying on familiar patterns. The limitation is that autopilot can preserve assumptions and responses that no longer fit the situation. A deliberate question interrupts the routine by asking why the process exists, what has changed, or what evidence would justify continuing it. The aim is not to question every ordinary action. It is to recognize situations where habit may conceal a meaningful problem or opportunity for change.
Study instruction:
- Identify a routine that is usually performed automatically and formulate a question that would reveal whether it still serves its intended purpose.
Key point:
- Automatic routines conserve attention but can preserve outdated assumptions.
- Deliberate questions are useful when a situation calls for reconsideration.
34. Q: When should people rely on routine and when should they deliberately question it?
A: Show answer
Routine is useful when a task is stable, well understood, low risk, and governed by a reliable procedure. It reduces repeated decisions and frees attention for more demanding work. Deliberate questioning becomes more important when outcomes deteriorate, conditions change, exceptions increase, stakeholders report problems, or the consequences of error become significant. A routine should not be abandoned merely because it is familiar, but familiarity is not proof that it remains appropriate. People can set review triggers such as recurring failures, new technology, regulatory changes, or changing user needs. This balances efficiency with the need to detect when established practice should be reconsidered.
Study instruction:
- Classify three routine activities by stability and risk, then define the conditions that should trigger a review of each one.
Key point:
- Routine can conserve effort when conditions remain stable.
- Change, exceptions, and poor outcomes should trigger renewed inquiry.
35. Q: How does questioning support critical thinking?
A: Show answer
Critical thinking requires more than collecting information or agreeing with a confident explanation. It involves examining what is being claimed, why it is being claimed, who is making the claim, what evidence supports it, and what alternative explanations may exist. Questions help make these checks explicit. The chapter identifies critical thinking as one of the expanded areas in the new edition and connects it to the ability to distinguish reliable information from misleading claims. The University of Sheffield describes critical thinking as a willingness to ask questions and evaluate evidence, assumptions, viewpoints, and implications. The objective is a reasoned judgment, not automatic skepticism toward every statement.
Study instruction:
- Select a claim and write questions about its meaning, author, evidence, assumptions, alternative viewpoints, and practical implications.
Key point:
- Critical thinking examines claims rather than accepting them at face value.
- Good questions make the reasoning behind a judgment more visible.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
36. Q: How can Socratic questioning help test a claim or assumption?
A: Show answer
Socratic questioning examines an idea through clarification, assumptions, evidence, viewpoints, implications, and the purpose of the question itself. It asks what a statement means, what it depends on, how it could be verified, what alternative views exist, and what follows if the claim is accepted. This method helps prevent a discussion from becoming a contest of confidence or authority. It also makes uncertainty visible: if evidence is weak or missing, the appropriate conclusion may be provisional rather than certain. The University of Sheffield presents Socratic questioning as a way to test claims and assumptions through structured evaluation rather than accepting information at face value.
Study instruction:
- Apply six Socratic question categories to a workplace proposal and record which claims remain unsupported after review.
Key point:
- Socratic questions test meaning, assumptions, evidence, viewpoints, and implications.
- A conclusion should reflect the strength of the available support.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
37. Q: What questions should be asked when evaluating the reliability of a source or piece of information?
A: Show answer
A careful evaluation asks who produced the information, what expertise or evidence supports it, why it was published, what perspective or interest may shape it, and how its conclusions were reached. The reader should check whether examples are representative, whether the evidence is sufficient, whether other reliable sources support the claim, and whether important limitations are disclosed. The date and context also matter because accurate information can become outdated or be applied beyond its original scope. These questions do not guarantee certainty, but they create a disciplined basis for deciding how much weight a source deserves and what additional verification is necessary.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate two sources about the same claim and compare authorship, purpose, evidence, methods, limitations, and corroboration.
Key point:
- Source reliability depends on authorship, purpose, evidence, and method.
- Important claims should be checked against relevant independent evidence.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
38. Q: How can questions help distinguish a fact from an interpretation or assumption?
A: Show answer
A fact is a statement that can be checked against evidence, while an interpretation explains what the fact might mean and an assumption is something accepted without sufficient examination. For example, a report showing that customer complaints increased is a factual observation if the data are reliable. Saying that the increase proves staff are careless is an interpretation that requires further evidence. Questions such as how the data were collected, what else changed, and what alternative explanations exist help separate these categories. This distinction reduces premature conclusions and encourages people to test explanations before making decisions or assigning responsibility.
Study instruction:
- Take a recent report and label its statements as observable facts, interpretations, or assumptions; identify how each interpretation could be tested.
Key point:
- Evidence, interpretation, and assumption should not be treated as interchangeable.
- Questions help test the reasoning that connects observations to conclusions.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
39. Q: How can questioning reduce the risk of accepting misleading or false information?
A: Show answer
Questions can slow the movement from exposure to belief. A reader should ask who is making the claim, what evidence is presented, whether the source has relevant expertise, whether the evidence can be independently checked, and what important context may be missing. It is also useful to ask what would disconfirm the claim and whether alternative explanations fit the facts. The chapter connects questioning with the challenge of distinguishing real information from fake news, but questioning alone does not identify truth automatically. It must be combined with source evaluation, corroboration, careful interpretation, and willingness to revise a conclusion when stronger evidence appears.
Study instruction:
- Choose a widely shared claim and verify its original source, evidence, context, date, and independent corroboration before deciding whether to accept it.
Key point:
- Questions interrupt uncritical acceptance of confident or widely repeated claims.
- Verification and corroboration are needed before reaching a conclusion.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
40. Q: What makes a question leading or loaded, and why can it weaken inquiry?
A: Show answer
A leading question signals the answer the questioner expects, while a loaded question embeds an assumption that may not be established. For example, asking why a team ignored an obvious warning assumes that the warning was obvious and that the team ignored it. Such wording can pressure people to accept the questioner's framing, discourage alternative explanations, and produce defensive or unreliable responses. A more neutral question would ask what information the team had at the time and how the warning was assessed. Neutral wording does not require pretending that all explanations are equally plausible; it keeps the inquiry open until evidence supports a conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite three leading questions as neutral prompts that distinguish observed events from assumptions about motives or responsibility.
Key point:
- Loaded wording can smuggle untested assumptions into an inquiry.
- Neutral questions make it easier to discover explanations the questioner did not anticipate.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
41. Q: Why does effective questioning matter for leadership?
A: Show answer
Leaders operate in environments where information is incomplete and the consequences of decisions may affect many people. A leader who assumes that having authority means having all the answers may miss emerging risks, suppress useful dissent, or continue relying on outdated assumptions. Effective questioning helps leaders clarify the problem, seek expertise, test strategic beliefs, and understand how decisions affect employees, customers, and communities. The chapter describes a need for leadership that can adapt to change rather than simply claim certainty. Questioning does not remove the leader's responsibility to decide; it improves the information and perspectives available before a decision is made.
Study instruction:
- Review a leadership decision and identify which questions would have exposed missing information, alternative views, or unintended consequences.
Key point:
- Leadership requires inquiry as well as direction and decision-making.
- A leader's questions can reveal whether the organization is learning or merely repeating assumptions.
Source links:
-
- INSEAD: The Innovator's DNA
42. Q: How can leaders create an environment where employees are willing to ask difficult questions?
A: Show answer
Leaders can make questioning safer by responding to challenges with curiosity rather than humiliation, explaining how decisions are evaluated, inviting alternative views, and showing that concerns receive fair consideration. They should distinguish respectful challenge from personal attack and make clear that employees are not punished merely for raising a legitimate issue. Formal channels are also needed for matters such as safety, fraud, harassment, or retaliation. Research on team psychological safety describes a shared belief that interpersonal risk-taking is safe and reports an association between psychological safety and learning behavior in work teams. A supportive climate does not remove accountability; it helps people communicate information needed for learning and correction.
Study instruction:
- Assess a team's response to dissent and identify specific leadership behaviors, safeguards, and reporting routes that affect employees' willingness to speak up.
Key point:
- Employees need evidence that questions and concerns will be considered fairly.
- Psychological safety supports learning behavior but does not replace accountability.
Source links:
-
- Edmondson: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
43. Q: What is psychological safety and how is it related to questioning at work?
A: Show answer
Psychological safety is a shared belief within a team that members can take interpersonal risks, such as asking for clarification, admitting an error, raising a concern, or challenging an assumption, without expecting humiliation or punishment for doing so. It does not mean that every idea must be accepted or that performance standards are relaxed. Rather, it supports the exchange of information needed to identify mistakes, learn, and improve work. Amy Edmondson's study of work teams found an association between team psychological safety and learning behavior. This provides an organizational explanation for why a culture that welcomes questions can matter alongside individual questioning skills.
Study instruction:
- Identify examples of interpersonal risk-taking in a team and evaluate how leaders respond when employees raise concerns or admit uncertainty.
Key point:
- Psychological safety concerns the perceived consequences of speaking up.
- It can support learning behavior while preserving clear standards and responsibilities.
Source links:
-
- Edmondson: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
44. Q: How can questioning improve communication and relationships with other people?
A: Show answer
Questions can shift a conversation from competing statements toward mutual understanding. Instead of preparing only to defend a position, a person can ask what the other person means, what experience shaped the view, what concern is most important, and what evidence or outcome would change the person's mind. The chapter's new material on interpersonal questioning emphasizes listening, follow-up questions, and rapport. These practices are useful because people often need to feel understood before they can consider another perspective. The goal is not to interrogate someone or manipulate agreement; it is to create a clearer exchange in which both participants can contribute information and reconsider assumptions.
Study instruction:
- In a conversation, ask a clarifying question, a follow-up question, and a perspective-seeking question before presenting your own conclusion.
Key point:
- Questions can build understanding by inviting the other person's perspective.
- Listening is necessary for questions to support genuine dialogue.
45. Q: What makes a follow-up question useful rather than repetitive or intrusive?
A: Show answer
A useful follow-up is connected to something the other person has just said and seeks clarification, an example, a reason, or an implication that has not yet been understood. It demonstrates listening because it responds to the person's actual answer rather than returning mechanically to a prepared list. For example, after someone describes a difficulty, asking which part created the greatest obstacle may reveal more than repeating the original question. Follow-ups should be paced respectfully and should not pressure someone to disclose information they do not wish to share. Their purpose is to deepen understanding while allowing the other person to retain agency in the conversation.
Study instruction:
- Practice building follow-up questions from a person's last answer and explain what additional understanding each question seeks.
Key point:
- Strong follow-ups respond to what was actually said.
- Respectful pacing and listening are as important as question wording.
46. Q: How can questioning help people engage across polarized viewpoints?
A: Show answer
In a polarized conversation, participants may focus on persuading, correcting, or defeating one another before they understand why the other person holds a particular view. Questions can create a different starting point by exploring the experiences, concerns, evidence, and values behind the position. A person might ask what outcome the other person is trying to protect or what information most influenced the view. This does not require accepting claims that are false or harmful. It means understanding the reasoning before responding, checking evidence fairly, and distinguishing the person from the proposition. Curiosity can make disagreement more informative without guaranteeing consensus.
Study instruction:
- Choose a disagreement and prepare questions that clarify the other person's concern, evidence, assumptions, and desired outcome without presuming agreement.
Key point:
- Inquiry can create understanding across disagreement without requiring agreement.
- Claims still need evidence-based evaluation and appropriate boundaries.
Source links:
-
- University of Sheffield: How to Think Critically
47. Q: How can a team use questions to improve the quality of a meeting or decision?
A: Show answer
A team can begin by clarifying the decision to be made, the problem it is intended to address, and the people affected. It can then ask what is known, what remains uncertain, which assumptions are driving the proposal, what alternatives deserve consideration, and what evidence would distinguish them. Before ending, the team should ask what decision has actually been made, what remains unresolved, who owns the next action, and when the evidence will be reviewed. This sequence reduces the risk of confusing discussion with agreement. It also ensures that questioning leads to accountable next steps rather than an unstructured exchange of opinions.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a meeting agenda that includes problem-framing questions, alternative-generation questions, evidence checks, and action-confirmation questions.
Key point:
- Questions improve meetings when they clarify both the decision and the unknowns.
- Every discussion should distinguish open issues from agreed actions.
Source links:
-
- Stanford d.school: How Might We Questions
48. Q: How can an individual build a regular habit of asking better questions?
A: Show answer
A practical habit can begin with a short daily or weekly inquiry period. The person selects a real problem or opportunity, writes questions without immediately judging them, and then reviews the list for assumptions, missing perspectives, and possible next steps. A question journal can preserve observations and show how the framing of a problem changes as new information appears. The chapter describes innovators who deliberately spend time generating questions and observing people outside their usual circles. A useful routine therefore combines written inquiry with listening, observation, and experiments rather than treating question generation as an isolated exercise.
Study instruction:
- Keep a question journal for one week and record the challenge, questions generated, evidence gathered, and any change in the original framing.
Key point:
- Questioning improves through deliberate practice and reflection.
- A journal can help reveal how new evidence changes the questions being asked.
Source links:
-
- INSEAD Knowledge: The Innovator's DNA
49. Q: How should a community organization apply Why/What If/How questioning to a persistent service problem?
A: Show answer
Suppose a community program has low participation. Why questions should examine who is not participating, when attendance declines, what barriers participants report, and whether the service is designed around assumptions rather than actual needs. What If questions could explore different locations, schedules, communication channels, delivery partners, or ways to involve participants in design. How questions would identify a small pilot, the people responsible, the resources required, the safeguards needed, and the measures that would indicate whether participation improves. The organization should involve affected people and avoid assuming that low attendance proves a lack of interest. The sequence turns a vague concern into an evidence-based improvement process.
Study instruction:
- Build a three-stage inquiry plan for a real service problem, including stakeholder questions, alternative designs, a small test, and review measures.
Key point:
- Start by understanding the problem from affected people's perspectives.
- Test alternatives on a manageable scale before committing to wider implementation.
Source links:
-
- Stanford d.school: Human-Centered Design Project Guide
50. Q: What are the most important lessons from Chapter 1 about the power of questioning?
A: Show answer
The chapter's central lesson is that meaningful questions can change how people understand problems and what they believe is possible. Questioning supports innovation, learning, critical thinking, leadership, and communication because it makes uncertainty visible and challenges assumptions that may otherwise remain unquestioned. Berger's Why/What If/How framework offers a practical path from examining the problem to imagining alternatives and taking action, while recognizing that inquiry is not a guaranteed formula. A beautiful question is ambitious enough to shift perspective and actionable enough to investigate. The wider discipline is to remain curious, evaluate evidence, listen to other people, and keep moving from questions toward learning and constructive change.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter using one example each of reframing with Why, exploring with What If, acting with How, and evaluating the result with further questions.
Key point:
- Better questions can open new understanding and make change possible.
- Inquiry is most valuable when curiosity, evidence, listening, and action work together.

Rows: 50
Table 2
The Surprising Power of Inquiry: Questioning, Innovation, and Action
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 2, The Surprising Power of Inquiry?
A: Show answer
Chapter 2 argues that questions are not merely requests for information; they are instruments for discovering problems, challenging assumptions, creating possibilities, and directing action. A well-formed question can expose an unsatisfactory condition that has been accepted as normal, reveal what is not yet understood, connect ideas from different fields, and motivate experiments. The chapter illustrates this through Van Phillips's effort to rethink prosthetic feet, the use of questions in science and education, and business and social innovations that began with noticing a gap. Its central practical message is that inquiry becomes transformative when curiosity is joined to ownership, disciplined exploration, and implementation. Asking alone may stimulate thought, but sustained questioning followed by action can change products, organizations, careers, and social arrangements.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter's argument in your own words, then identify one example each of a question that discovers a problem, opens a possibility, and initiates action."
Key point:
- Questions convert curiosity into structured inquiry and can challenge accepted limits.
- Innovation requires more than asking: it requires taking responsibility and acting on what inquiry reveals.
2. Q: Why did Van Phillips ask, If they can put a man on the moon, why can't they make a decent foot?
A: Show answer
After losing his lower left leg in a boating accident in 1976, Phillips received a rudimentary prosthesis that performed poorly. Tripping over a small pebble convinced him that simply being told to accept the device was not an adequate response to the problem. At the same time, he knew that advanced engineering was being applied to ambitious projects such as the space program. He therefore questioned why comparable ingenuity could not improve a practical, immediate human need. The question exposed a contrast between technological capability and the quality of an everyday solution. It also reframed his situation from passive adjustment to an opportunity to investigate whether prosthetic design could be fundamentally improved.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the contrast between the space program and the prosthesis available to Phillips, and identify the assumption his question challenged."
Key point:
- A concrete failure in an ordinary product can expose a much larger innovation opportunity.
- A powerful question compares what is currently available with what existing knowledge and capabilities might make possible.
3. Q: What does refusing to accept the existing reality mean in innovative inquiry?
A: Show answer
It means treating current arrangements as conditions that can be examined rather than as permanent limits. Phillips accepted the fact that he had become an amputee, but he rejected the idea that he must accept an ineffective prosthetic foot. That distinction matters: productive questioning does not deny facts that cannot be changed; it asks whether the response, system, product, or convention surrounding those facts can be improved. Refusing the status quo is not automatically evidence that an alternative will work. It is the starting point for investigation, learning, and testing. A questioner must remain open to evidence that confirms or disproves the proposed improvement.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a familiar process or product that people accept as inevitable and write a question that separates unavoidable facts from changeable design choices."
Key point:
- Question the arrangement that can be changed without denying the reality that cannot.
- Challenging the status quo creates an opening for inquiry; evidence must determine what should replace it.
4. Q: How did the prosthetics industry's lack of investment create an opportunity for Phillips?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that prosthetics had experienced decades of limited progress because the market was not considered commercially attractive. The relatively small customer base gave governments and large companies little incentive to invest heavily in innovation. This neglect left outdated assumptions, materials, and designs open to challenge. Phillips's opportunity therefore arose partly from a gap between the importance of the users' needs and the attention the market gave them. A neglected market is not automatically easy to enter: technical requirements, clinical suitability, cost, safety, and user acceptance still matter. The lesson is that low innovation activity may indicate an underserved need, but a questioner must investigate why the gap exists and what would make a viable solution possible.
Study instruction:
- "Identify an underserved user group in a market and investigate the commercial, technical, institutional, or cultural reasons its needs receive limited attention."
Key point:
- Neglected needs can leave room for innovation when existing solutions are inadequate.
- Market neglect may reveal opportunity, but it also signals constraints that a solution must address.
5. Q: Why can an outsider sometimes ask questions that established experts overlook?
A: Show answer
An outsider may be less invested in the assumptions, routines, terminology, and professional boundaries that shape an established field. Phillips initially approached prosthetics without accepting that its traditional forms were the only reasonable options. This distance allowed him to ask basic questions that specialists might have stopped considering. The chapter also warns that outsiders do not automatically know more than experts. Phillips had to enter the field, study prosthetics, work in a laboratory, and understand why existing designs had developed as they had. The productive combination is fresh perspective plus serious learning. Outsider questions are most valuable when they expose assumptions and then become informed through contact with the actual problem.
Study instruction:
- "Examine a problem outside your area of expertise. List assumptions an outsider might question and the specialist knowledge needed to evaluate those questions responsibly."
Key point:
- Distance from established assumptions can stimulate fresh inquiry.
- An outsider perspective is a starting advantage, not a substitute for domain knowledge.
6. Q: How can expertise sometimes weaken questioning?
A: Show answer
Expertise can make a person fluent in established explanations and procedures, which is useful for reliable performance but can also make familiar assumptions feel unquestionable. When someone believes that they already know how a problem works, they may stop looking for alternative explanations or designs. The chapter uses the observation that an expert may have stopped thinking because they know to illustrate this risk. The point is not that expertise is harmful or that novices should disregard specialists. Expertise becomes limiting when knowledge is treated as final rather than provisional. Effective experts preserve the habit of testing assumptions, noticing anomalies, and updating their understanding when evidence or circumstances change.
Study instruction:
- "Identify an area in which your experience may cause you to make assumptions automatically. Write two questions that would test whether those assumptions remain valid."
Key point:
- Knowledge is valuable, but confidence in existing knowledge can close off inquiry.
- Strong expertise combines technical competence with intellectual humility and continued questioning.
7. Q: Why is changing the pronoun from they to I important in the chapter's account of inquiry?
A: Show answer
The change from they to I represents a shift from expecting an unnamed outside party to solve a problem toward accepting personal responsibility for exploring it. Phillips first asked why they could not make a better foot, but he eventually recognized that he would have to investigate the problem himself. Mark Noonan's improved snow shovel provides a parallel example: rather than continuing to complain that someone should invent a better tool, he designed one. Taking ownership does not mean one person must perform every task or possess every resource. It means accepting responsibility for the next useful step, such as learning, assembling collaborators, testing an idea, or finding support.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a complaint about a service, workflow, or product using I-language that identifies one action you can take without assuming you control the entire problem."
Key point:
- Ownership changes inquiry from passive expectation into purposeful participation.
- Taking responsibility means initiating a constructive next step, not claiming sole control over every outcome.
8. Q: What is the difference between questioning and complaining?
A: Show answer
Questioning investigates why a condition exists, what could be different, and how a change might be achieved. Complaining may identify dissatisfaction but stops short of taking responsibility for learning or action. The distinction is not whether a person expresses frustration; frustration can reveal an important unmet need. The distinction is whether the person uses that dissatisfaction to investigate causes, explore alternatives, and test a response. Noonan's shovel illustrates the transition: the question about a better shovel became meaningful when he designed a practical alternative. A complaint can therefore become the beginning of inquiry, but it becomes productive only when it is converted into a specific, answerable line of investigation.
Study instruction:
- "Take one recurring complaint and turn it into three questions: what is failing, why does it fail, and what could you test to improve it?"
Key point:
- Complaints describe dissatisfaction; productive questions investigate and mobilize change.
- A useful question creates a path toward evidence or action rather than merely repeating frustration.
9. Q: How did Phillips turn his personal question into a sustained inquiry?
A: Show answer
He recognized that meaningful progress required understanding the prosthetics field rather than relying only on dissatisfaction with the device he had received. Although his original academic background was in broadcasting, he enrolled in a prosthetics study program and later worked in a prosthetics laboratory. This gave him exposure to materials, design practices, technical constraints, and the reasons existing devices were built as they were. He continued to challenge conventional assumptions while developing enough knowledge to make his questions more precise. The process took years and involved repeated redesign. His example shows that ownership of a question creates a learning obligation: the questioner must enter the relevant context, acquire knowledge, and refine the problem as understanding improves.
Study instruction:
- "Map the learning steps required to investigate a problem outside your current expertise, including formal study, observation, practitioner interviews, and practical experience."
Key point:
- Serious inquiry requires immersion in the context of the problem.
- Curiosity becomes more effective when it is supported by deliberate learning and contact with real practice.
10. Q: Why did Phillips's repeated prototype failures contribute to progress?
A: Show answer
Each broken prototype produced information about the limits of the current design. Rather than treating failure only as defeat, Phillips examined why the foot had broken, considered changes to materials or structure, and tested a revised version. This created a learning cycle in which each test narrowed uncertainty and informed the next design. Failure is useful only when it is observed, analyzed, and converted into a decision; repeating the same failed experiment without learning wastes time and resources. The chapter's account therefore supports disciplined experimentation rather than romanticizing failure. The goal is not to fail frequently but to make tests informative, recognize what the results show, and improve the next iteration.
Study instruction:
- "Design a small experiment for a current problem. State the hypothesis, the test, the evidence to record, and how each possible result would affect the next iteration."
Key point:
- An informative failure can reduce uncertainty and improve the next attempt.
- Failure produces learning only when the result is analyzed and used to change the next test.
11. Q: What does it mean to describe questions as engines of intellect?
A: Show answer
The metaphor emphasizes that questions can convert curiosity into controlled inquiry. Curiosity may begin as a vague sense that something is interesting or unsatisfactory. A question gives that curiosity direction by identifying what needs explanation, what information is missing, or what possibility deserves exploration. The chapter also uses metaphors such as a flashlight that illuminates where to go, a spade that uncovers buried information, and a lever that opens a stuck lid. These images highlight different functions: directing attention, uncovering assumptions, and creating movement where thinking has become stuck. The practical point is that questions are active tools for structuring thought rather than decorative expressions of interest.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the engine, flashlight, spade, and lever metaphors. For a real problem, decide which function a question needs to perform first."
Key point:
- Questions give curiosity direction and turn it into purposeful investigation.
- A good question can focus attention, uncover hidden information, or loosen a fixed assumption.
12. Q: Can a question exist before it is spoken aloud?
A: Show answer
Yes. The chapter distinguishes questioning as a mental activity from the act of verbally asking another person. A person can carry an unresolved question internally for a long time, use it to guide observation, and gradually refine it before sharing it. This matters because inquiry may begin with private curiosity, reflection, or noticing a mismatch between expectation and reality. Speaking the question can make it available for collaboration, criticism, and new information, but speech is not what creates the underlying question. A private question can also remain unproductive if it is never examined or acted upon. The important distinction is between the existence of inquiry and the communication of inquiry to others.
Study instruction:
- "Keep a short record of questions that arise during work or study. Later, select one and refine it into a question that another person could investigate with you."
Key point:
- Inquiry can begin internally before it becomes a conversation.
- Writing or sharing a question can make it more precise and open it to collaboration.
13. Q: Why is awareness of what we do not know a foundation of effective questioning?
A: Show answer
A person can investigate a gap only after recognizing that something is unknown, uncertain, or insufficiently explained. The chapter describes good questioners as people who are comfortable acknowledging their ignorance and using it as a guide to inquiry. This does not mean celebrating a lack of knowledge or abandoning standards of competence. It means accurately recognizing the boundary between what is known, what is assumed, and what remains unexplored. Such awareness prevents premature certainty and helps identify where evidence or expertise is needed. In science, business, and everyday problem-solving, the willingness to say I do not yet know can be the beginning of a better question and a more reliable answer.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a topic you think you understand. Separate your knowledge into verified facts, assumptions, and unanswered questions, then identify how to investigate the largest gap."
Key point:
- Recognizing the limits of knowledge creates a map for further inquiry.
- Intellectual humility is not weakness; it is a condition for learning and updating beliefs.
14. Q: How can questions create new fields of investigation while answers sometimes close inquiry?
A: Show answer
A well-formed question can reveal several layers of uncertainty, suggest new lines of research, and challenge an established way of thinking. One answer may resolve a particular issue, but it can also expose further questions about causes, implications, exceptions, or applications. The chapter contrasts the expansive potential of questions with the tendency of an answer to end a particular line of inquiry. This is not a claim that answers are unimportant. Answers are needed to test explanations and guide decisions. The point is that inquiry should not stop merely because an initial answer has been found. A useful answer often becomes the basis for a more refined question.
Study instruction:
- "After answering a question in a subject you are studying, write at least three follow-up questions that examine implications, exceptions, or unresolved causes."
Key point:
- Questions can multiply avenues of inquiry beyond the first answer.
- Treat answers as provisional steps in learning when the evidence or problem remains incomplete.
15. Q: What does the Right Question Institute example suggest about teaching and learning?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes classroom exercises in which learners are asked to brainstorm using only questions. This changes the learner's role from receiving information to actively generating lines of inquiry. According to the account, question-only brainstorming can increase engagement and help ideas emerge before participants settle on a particular answer. The example suggests that questioning can be taught as a deliberate thinking practice rather than left to spontaneous curiosity. It also implies that a teacher can design activities that give learners permission to explore uncertainties, formulate possibilities, and then use their questions to focus further work. The exercise is not a replacement for subject knowledge; it is a way to make learners more active in using and extending that knowledge.
Study instruction:
- "Run a short question-only brainstorming exercise on a topic, then group the questions into themes and select those that can be investigated with available evidence."
Key point:
- Question generation can make learners active participants in constructing understanding.
- Teaching people how to ask can deepen engagement and improve the direction of subsequent learning.
16. Q: How do divergent and convergent thinking contribute to questioning?
A: Show answer
Divergent thinking expands the range of possibilities by generating alternatives, associations, and what-if ideas. Convergent thinking narrows that range by examining evidence, identifying the central issue, comparing options, and deciding how to proceed. The chapter explains that questioning can support both modes: broad questions open imaginative exploration, while more focused questions help participants clarify a problem and reach a decision. Effective inquiry does not remain permanently in either mode. Excessive divergence can produce many ideas without a practical choice, while premature convergence can eliminate promising possibilities before they are examined. A useful process deliberately creates space for alternatives and then applies criteria to select and develop them.
Study instruction:
- "Take a problem through two separate rounds: first generate as many plausible questions and options as possible, then use evidence and explicit criteria to narrow them."
Key point:
- Divergent thinking expands possibilities; convergent thinking focuses and evaluates them.
- Good inquiry moves between openness and disciplined selection rather than relying on only one mode.
17. Q: What is metacognitive questioning, and why is it useful?
A: Show answer
Metacognitive questioning means examining the questions one is asking and the thinking process behind them. Instead of only asking about the external problem, a learner asks whether the question is clear, whether it contains an assumption, what evidence would answer it, and whether it is leading toward a useful outcome. The chapter presents this as a sophisticated use of inquiry because people can use questions to reflect on their own questions. This helps identify vague wording, hidden biases, premature narrowing, and unproductive lines of investigation. Metacognition is especially useful when a group is stuck or when an apparently precise question repeatedly produces irrelevant answers. Reviewing the question may reveal that the real problem has not yet been framed correctly.
Study instruction:
- "Review one important question using four checks: clarity, assumptions, evidence needed, and usefulness for the decision you face."
Key point:
- Questioning can be directed at the quality of one's own inquiry.
- Improving the question may be more valuable than collecting more answers to a poorly framed question.
18. Q: Why does the chapter describe questioning as an egalitarian activity?
A: Show answer
A powerful question does not require formal authority, seniority, or specialist status. A person who is closest to a problem, affected by a service, or willing to notice an inconsistency may ask something that redirects an entire investigation. Phillips's position as a user of prosthetics helped him see shortcomings that established practitioners had learned to tolerate. The chapter also notes that questioning can be difficult for leaders because acknowledging uncertainty may be socially uncomfortable. The egalitarian character of inquiry does not remove differences in expertise or decision rights. It means that the right to raise a useful question should not be restricted to people with the highest status. Organizations benefit when relevant questions can come from any level.
Study instruction:
